WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Resilient activity and simmering inflation
While bumpy and uneven, New Zealand’s economic
recovery has continued over the past few months, with
activity showing resilience in the face of continued global
headwinds. Under the surface, however, inflation pressures

are bubbling away, with much of that pressure centred on
parts of the economy that aren’t sensitive to interest rates.
Economic activity getting back on firmer footing
Looking first at recent activity data, the economy has been
finding its feet again after taking a knock in the wake of the
Middle East war. That has been seen most clearly in the jobs
market, with the number of filled jobs rising by 0.9% in the
year to August. While not shooting the lights out, that is still a

noticeable improvement from the early part of the year, when
filled job numbers were falling.
Under the surface, it’s still a very mixed picture. Agriculture
remains the main engine of growth, with the strength in
commodity export earnings helping to boost incomes,
confidence and spending in regions with strong rural backbones.
That’s supported firm job growth in regions like Canterbury
(where filled job numbers are up 1.9% over the past year), Otago

(+1.5%) and Southland (+3.1%). In contrast, jobs growth remains
much more moderate in Auckland (up 0.6%) and Wellington
(up just 0.4%). That town-and-country split is likely to remain a
feature of the economic landscape for some time yet.
Looking ahead, businesses expect trading conditions to
remain resilient through the final months of the year. That
was reflected in September’s Business Outlook survey.
While the survey’s gauges of expected business activity and

general business confidence eased over the past month, they
remain at firm levels, consistent with continued moderate
growth through the latter part of the year. That resilience has
also been evident in the household sector, with consumer
confidence largely steady through September.
The resilience in household and business sentiment is notable,
as fuel prices have taken a big step higher over the past month.
The average price of 91 unleaded has risen by around 49c/litre –

albeit late in the month and likely not fully reflected in surveyed
confidence – taking it back to the levels seen during the early
stages of the Middle East conflict. Those higher fuel prices
have taken a bite out of household disposable incomes, with
spending on fuel up about 22% compared with this time last
year, even though the volume of fuel sold is down around 11%.
But even with the lift in fuel prices, we haven’t seen the same
degree of pessimism as earlier in the year, when fuel prices first

spiked. Notably, those higher fuel prices haven’t spilled over
into a broader lift in output price pressures either (at least at
this stage). Inflation expectations were steady in September’s
household and business surveys. Similarly, the number of firms
planning to raise prices fell back despite a lift in wage and cost
expectations. However, while that stability in pricing gauges is
welcome, measures of inflation pressure remain elevated.
Taken together, recent data reinforce indications that the

economy is shaking off the impacts of the Iran war.
Persistent inflation drivers and the RBNZ
Turning to inflation, the spike in oil prices earlier this year saw
annual inflation climb to 4.1% in the year to June. With local
fuel costs rising again in recent weeks, we continue to expect
inflation to remain above 3% until at least mid-2027.
However, even before the recent rise in fuel costs, inflation had
been running hot for an extended period. Excluding the rise

in food and fuel prices, inflation in the year to June was 2.9%,
and it’s been running close to or above 3% for five years now.
As we discuss in our recent report, much of the strength in
inflation over the past year has been related to administered
costs, such as local council rates, or to sectors that are less
exposed to competitive pressures, such as electricity and
insurance. Those increases have persisted for an extended period,
and we expect further sizeable increases in many of these costs.

This ongoing firmness is a key reason why we expect domestic
(non-tradables) inflation to be higher than the RBNZ expects
over the longer term. It’s also part of the reason why we
expect the RBNZ will ultimately need to raise the OCR by
more than it assumed in its September policy statement.
We are forecasting a 25bp rate rise at the RBNZ’s December
meeting and expect the OCR to peak at 4% next year.
This is a tough environment for the RBNZ. With ongoing

increases in administered prices boosting core inflation at a
time when energy prices are driving headline inflation well
above the target range, it’s hard for the RBNZ to look through
them. The RBNZ had little headroom to absorb upside inflation
surprises, even before the recent spike in oil prices.
Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
But getting inflation back to target in the face of an oil price
spike and high administered prices will not be easy and involves
some tough trade-offs. Administered prices tend to be less
responsive to changes in economic conditions. As a result,
interest rate hikes are likely to have a bigger impact on parts of

the economy that are not the main drivers of inflation. Tighter
policy will also weigh on the labour market and near-term
growth, while increasing households’ debt-servicing costs.
This is an uncomfortable but necessary trade-off. Looking
through, or not responding to, these sorts of large and persistent
cost increases would still leave households facing significant
increases in overall living costs. In the long run, it would be
more damaging for households and the economy if generalised

inflation were to become embedded in price setting behaviour.
The Kiwi has lost its wings
Adding to near-term inflation risks has been the drop in the NZ
dollar. On a trade-weighted basis, the Kiwi has fallen around
4% since the start of September, and it’s begun the December
quarter around 4% below the level the RBNZ assumed in its
September policy statement.
The slide in the Kiwi is mainly due to the strength of the US
dollar, with NZD/USD down nearly 5% since the start of

September to sit just below 0.56 at the time of writing. Moves
against other currencies have been more modest. For example,
NZD/AUD is down 2% over the same period.
Technically, NZD/USD seems oversold. In the short term,
trends in the US dollar will dominate, including any response
to the US labour market report due on Friday evening. Any
turnaround in the USD would likely see NZD/USD trade back
towards the top of the 0.5580–0.5680 range that is governing

the pair for the time being.
NZD/AUD will continue to be driven by changing views on
interest rate differentials between the RBNZ and RBA. This
week, NZD/AUD corrected higher from around 0.8070 to as high
as 0.8110 as RBA cash rate expectations were repriced following
the RBA Board meeting and Australian CPI. In the short term, the
bias for NZD/AUD could be lower, given our view that the RBNZ
will pause at its late-October meeting and that the market is

under-pricing the risk of an RBA rate hike in November.
Next week’s QSBO, due on Tuesday, will be the key event
risk, with the inflation and growth gauges closely watched for
signs that might prompt the RBNZ to pause or hike. Markets
had previously priced in a high chance of a hike at the RBNZ’s
October meeting, but in recent days appear to be reassessing
that view.  Pricing for a 25bp hike in New Zealand could be
pared back towards a 50:50 call. The 0.8030–0.8150 range

is likely to contain the pair in the immediate period ahead.
Our medium-term view is for NZD/AUD to rise as the RBNZ
increases the OCR to 4% by the second half of 2027. We’ll
update our views on the NZD and other currencies in our next
Market Outlook, out Wednesday 14 October.
Fiscal update
The big uncertainty on the domestic horizon is, of course,
November’s general election, and at this stage the polling
looks tight. Neither the established left nor right bloc has a

clear majority.
Ahead of the election, the Treasury has released its Pre-election
Economic and Fiscal Update (PREFU), updating the Budget
2026 (BEFU) fiscal forecasts to reflect the latest economic and
fiscal information and a revised economic outlook. This allows
the public to assess the state of the Government’s finances. It
also provides other parties with a baseline against which to
calibrate their spending and taxation policies.
The PREFU revealed a stronger starting point for the

Government’s finances than had been expected in the Budget.
• A deficit of $8.6bn is estimated for 2025/26, down from the
Budget estimate of $11.9bn.
• Similarly, the forecast deficit for 2026/27 has been revised
down significantly to $6.8bn from $11.4bn.
• Thereafter, the OBEGALx deficit is expected to continue
declining gradually, moving to a surplus of $4bn in 2028/29.
This broadly follows the Budget profile, but is stronger
across the forecast period.

• Net core Crown debt is forecast to peak at 43.9% of GDP in
2027/28, below the 46.1% peak forecast in the BEFU. It’s then
forecast to decline to 39.5% of GDP by the end of the forecast
period in 2030/31, in line with the Government’s goal of
reducing debt below 40% of GDP over the medium term.
Given the improved outlook, NZDM announced a $4bn
reduction in the 2026/27 bond programme to $30bn and a
$15bn reduction in the forecast programme across the whole

period through to 2029/30.
The medium-term forecasts in the PREFU should be treated
with caution. The economic forecasts underpinning the
PREFU were finalised on 21 August, when both oil prices
and, especially, bond yields were materially lower than they
are today. Had the forecasts been finalised more recently, the
fiscal projections presented today would have been weaker,
at least in the near term. Indeed, the Treasury is upfront in
acknowledging that, while the risks are two-sided, the balance

is skewed to the downside.
Aside from the obvious uncertainty surrounding the economic
outlook because of unpredictable developments in the Middle
East, the approaching General Election, and the possibility of
a change in the composition of the Government, means the
assumptions underpinning the PREFU forecasts could quickly
become invalidated.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Oct 6, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment declined 5.2% to 84.4 in September,
taking the index back towards the deeply pessimistic levels seen
earlier in the year. Rising fuel prices and fears of an imminent

interest rate rise drove the move with a continued weakening in
housing markets likely adding to unease in some quarters.
Developments over the last month would have added to these
concerns with: the RBA raising the cash rate by 25bps to 4.6%
during the survey week; average pump prices pushing above
$2.36/litre nationally and no end in sight to global supply
disruptions;  and more weak updates on the housing market.
NZ: Q2 NZIER Survey of Business Opinion (index)

Oct 6, General business confidence last: 12.3
Business conditions picked up modestly in the June quarter as
concerns about the war in the Middle East eased. However,
cost and pricing gauges remained firm. The September quarter
survey will be closely watched ahead of the RBNZ’s 28
October meeting. Recent weeks have seen signs that activity
is firming. However, fuel prices have also risen sharply, and we
(along with the RBNZ) will be watching for signs of a broader

lift in inflation pressures. The timing of survey responses
within the sample period will also warrant scrutiny, given that
oil prices rose late in the quarter.

Chỉ số, nhóm ngành, khối ngoại và phái sinh

Top 5 ngành tăng / giảm  VNINDEX giảm điểm trong tuần qua, -30.55 điểm tương ứng -1.68%, với thanh khoản suy 
giảm. Dệt may, Tiện ích khí và Bao bì & đóng gói là các nhóm ngành có mức giảm rõ rệt 
nhất trong tuần. Khối ngoại đảo chiều bán ròng so với xu hướng của tuần trước với giá trị  
-2,865 tỷ đồng, tập trung ở các mã VHM, VPB và VIC; đồng thời, tự doanh đảo chiều bán 
ròng ở mức -146 tỷ đồng.

Ở một khía cạnh khác, khối ngoại bán ròng -3,351 HĐTL VN30F1M trong tuần qua nhưng 
đang duy trì vị thế mua lũy kế 1,650 hợp đồng. Tự doanh mua ròng 2,822 hợp đồng nhưng 
đang duy trì trạng thái lũy kế bán ròng -6,636 hợp đồng.

TTCK Việt Nam bước vào tuần đầu tiên ở vị thế thị trường mới nổi bằng một nhịp điều 
chỉnh, khi VN-Index để mất mốc 1.800 điểm và lùi về đóng cửa quanh 1.785 điểm. Đây là 
phản ứng "mua kỳ vọng, bán thực tế" khá điển hình: ngay trong phiên nâng hạng chính 
thức 21/9, chỉ số mở cửa hưng phấn rồi nhanh chóng đảo chiều do áp lực chốt lời, với 
nhóm cổ phiếu Vingroup (VIC, VHM, VRE) giảm mạnh dẫn dắt đà đi xuống, và sức ép lan 
rộng sang bất động sản, ngân hàng, năng lượng về cuối tuần. Điểm đáng chú ý là dòng 
vốn ngoại đã tiếp tục bán ròng ngay sau cột mốc ngày 21/9, dù đã mua ròng trở lại trong 
tuần trước đó. Bối cảnh bên ngoài cũng kém thuận lợi khi Fed vừa tăng lãi suất và phát đi 
tín hiệu diều hâu, chứng khoán Mỹ điều chỉnh, giá dầu neo cao, và lợi suất trái phiếu 
Chính phủ Mỹ tăng vọt. Trong một, hai tuần tới, bên cạnh các yếu tố ngoại biên, mùa báo 
cáo KQKD quý 3 cũng sẽ bắt đầu tác động lên diễn biến thị trường chung.

Lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ tăng 
trưởng khả quan trong 2Q26 14% 
YoY, với động lực chính đến từ mảng 
dịch vụ CNTT nước ngoài

Mảng CNTT nước ngoài: Lĩnh vực dịch 
vụ IT phân cực mạnh mẽ do tác nhân 
AI (AI Agentic) s ẽ củng c ố xu hư ớng 
đầu tư vào AI

FPT ở vị thế thuận lợi cho phép 
doanh nghiệp đa dạng hóa công cụ 
AI và tối ưu chi phí vận hành

Khuyến ngh ị MUA với giá m ục tiêu 
86,000 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Do không còn h ạch toán FPT Telecom (FOX) là công ty con, d oanh thu 2Q2026 của 
FPT giảm mạnh còn 13,810 tỷ VND (-17% YoY). Lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ không 
bị ảnh hưởng bởi thay đổi hạch toán đạt 2,568 tỷ VND (14% YoY). Mảng dịch vụ CNTT 
tại thị trường nước ngoài tiếp tục là đ ộng lực tăng trư ởng chính, đạt doanh thu và 
LNTT lần lượt là 9,868/1,538 tỷ VND (16.3% /19.3% YoY).

Sự chuyển dịch chi tiêu từ dịch vụ IT truyền thống sang chuyển đổi số và tích hợp AI 
được kỳ vọng sẽ diễn ra ngày m ột mạnh mẽ khi: i) Sự phát triển của năng l ực tính 
toán giúp kéo giảm chi phí token (đơn v ị năng lực tính toán của AI) và thúc đ ẩy nhu 
cầu sử dụng AI của doanh nghiệp; ii) Các mô hình AI mã nguồn mở gia tăng lựa chọn 
cho người dùng sẽ thúc đẩy chi tiêu

Đối với các doanh nghiệp ứng dụng sâu các mô hình AI phục vụ hoạt động kinh doanh 
như FPT , chúng tôi nhận thấy xu hướng tối ưu chi phí tốt hơn nhờ khả năng sử dụng 
các mô hình AI đ ến từ đa dạng nhà cung c ấp; với chi phí trên hi ệu năng tính toán 
được cải thi ện. Trong k ịch b ản cơ s ở, chúng tôi d ự báo biên l ợi nhuận trước thu ế 
mảng CNTT nước ngoài sẽ ổn định ở mức 15.4% nhờ duy trì chi ến lược quản trị chi 
phí hiệu quả qua đó giúp LNTT 2026/2027 đạt 6,198/7,087 tỷ VND (13.3%/14.3% YoY).

Dựa trên định giá FCFF và P/E, triển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các rủi ro có 
thể phát sinh, chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu FPT . Giá mục tiêu là 86,000 
VND/cổ phiếu.

Các mảng kinh doanh duy trì ổn định 
giúp lợi nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ 
tăng trưởng nhẹ

Sản lượng điện của REE tăng trưởng 
nhẹ nhờ mức tăng của mảng điện gió 
sau khi dự án Duyên Hải V1-4 vận 
hành

Mảng M&E ghi nhận giá trị ký mới cao 
trong 1H.2026, t ạo kỳ vọng hồi phục 
mạnh mẽ doanh thu

Mảng cho thuê văn phòng đứng trước 
cơ hội lớn đóng góp vào tăng trư ởng 
nhờ các điều kiện thuận lợi

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
58,100 VNĐ/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu Quý 2/2026 giảm nhẹ, đạt 2,432 tỷ VND (-3% YoY/-2% QoQ), trong khi l ợi 
nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ đạt 632 tỷ VND (+1% YoY/-11.5% QoQ), do doanh thu của 
mảng bất động sản suy giảm. Qua đó, REE hoàn thành lần lượt 40%/47.8% kế hoạch 
doanh thu và LNST công ty mẹ 2026.

Trong Q2.2026, tổng sản lượng phát đi ện của REE tăng nh ẹ, đạt 2.8 tỷ kWh (+2.3% 
YoY) với đóng góp tăng trưởng rõ nét đến từ mảng điện gió khi dự án Duyên Hải V1-4 
bắt đầu vận hành; tuy nhiên  sản lượng thủy điện suy gi ảm do tác đ ộng tiêu c ực từ 
pha thời tiết El Nino, đạt 1.1 tỷ kWh.

Với năng lực chuyên môn được nâng cao, mảng M&E ghi nhận những dấu hiệu phục 
hồi tích cực trong giá trị hợp đồng ký mới trong 1H.2026, 5,865 tỷ VND (cao hơn 53.9% 
so với giá trị ký mới cả năm 2025). KBSV dự phóng doanh thu c ủa mảng M&E sẽ đạt 
4,499/5,190 tỷ VND (+28%/15% YoY) trong 2026/2027.

Hội tụ bởi 3 yếu tố: i) Định hướng phát triển kinh tế Hồ Chí Minh trở thành trung tâm 
tài chính quốc tế; ii) Hạ tầng giao thông ngày một được hoàn thiện và iii) Chi phí thuê 
cạnh tranh so với chất lượng dịch vụ của E. Town 6, KBSV dự phóng doanh thu mảng 
cho thuê văn phòng trong 2026/2027 tăng trưởng +14%/6.2% YoY.

Dựa trên đ ịnh giá SOTP , triển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các r ủi ro có th ể 
phát sinh, chúng tôi khuy ến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu REE. Giá m ục tiêu là 58,100 
VND/cổ phiếu, cao hơn 28.3% so với mức giá đóng cửa tại ngày 15/09/2026.

Doanh thu 2Q.26 bứt phá nhờ khoản 
thu nhập bất thường của nhiệt điện 
Cà Mau và Vũng Áng

Nhiệt đi ện than duy trì công su ất ở 
mức cao nh ờ nguồn cung nhiên li ệu 
ổn định trong 2H26

Điện khí Đông Nam Bộ (NT1&2 và 
NT3&4) có triển vọng hoạt động 
thuận lợi

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
18,100 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Trong Quý 2/2026, POW ghi nhận Doanh thu và LNST cổ đông công ty mẹ đạt lần lượt 
20,308 tỷ VND (116.1% YoY) và 3,519 tỷ VND (484.6% YoY) chủ yếu nhờ các khoản thu 
nhập từ chênh lệch tiền khí của nhiệt điện Cà Mau và tỷ giá của nhiệt điện Vũng Áng. 
Với kết qu ả trên, POW hoàn thành l ần lư ợt 65%/420% kế hoạch doanh thu và l ợi 
nhuận sau thuế công ty mẹ.

Chúng tôi cho r ằng nhà máy nhi ệt than Vũng Áng trong 2026 s ẽ được duy trì công 
suất huy động ở mức cao nhờ: i) Xác suất diễn ra El Nino tăng cao và dự kiến kéo dài 
đến đầu 2027, ii) Nguồn cung than trong nước được bảo đảm từ các đối tác (TKV, TCT 
Đông B ắc) và d ự trữ than d ồi dào c ủa doanh nghi ệp ghi nh ận ở mức cao trong 
Q2/2026.

Dự án đường dây 500kV Mạch 3 đi vào vận hành giúp giảm áp lực cạnh tranh của các 
nhà máy điện tại khu vực phía Nam, qua đó kỳ vọng doanh thu của NT1&2 trong năm 
2026 đạt 11,104 tỷ VND (5.3% YoY). Đối với dự án điện NT3&4, khoản thu nhập chênh 
lệch sản lượng theo giá công suất do sản lượng điện thực tế thấp hơn so với tỷ lệ Qc 
65% của nhà máy được duy trì sang 2H26 và 2027 sẽ giúp cho dự án ghi nhận doanh 
thu lần lượt 17,848/17,621 tỷ VND doanh thu trong 2026/2027.

Dựa trên định giá SOTP và EV/EBITDA, tri ển vọng kinh doanh cũng như xem xét các 
rủi ro có th ể phát sinh, chúng tôi khuy ến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu POW. Giá m ục 
tiêu là 18,100 VND/cổ phiếu.

tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu

2Q2026, AST ghi nh ận doanh thu và LNST đ ạt 479/92 t ỷ VND (+22%/19% yoy), với 
động lực chủ yếu đến từ (1) đóng góp từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn 
Nhất và (2) tăng trưởng tích cực của các mảng hàng miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood 
và phòng ch ờ thương gia. Lũy k ế 6T2026, doanh thu và LNST đ ạt 1,035/221 t ỷ VND 
(+33%/46% yoy), lần lượt hoàn thành 48%/49% kế hoạch năm.

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa tại các thị trường AST khai thác tăng 
bình quân 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. Lưu lượng và tỷ trọng khách quốc tế gia tăng 
được kỳ vọng thúc đẩy số giao dịch và doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi khách, đặc biệt 
tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

Chúng tôi dự phóng số điểm kinh doanh AST tăng từ 145 điểm cuối năm 2026 lên 182 
điểm năm 2030. Sau đóng góp t ừ 19 điểm tại T3 Tân Sơn Nh ất và 6 điểm mới tại T2 
Nội Bài từ 2025 - 2026, các dự án Long Thành, Phú Qu ốc và Gia Bình ti ếp tục mở ra 
dư địa mở rộng mạng lưới trong trung và dài hạn cho doanh nghiệp.

Việc mở rộng các hoạt động mang lại lợi nhuận cao, bao gồm hàng miễn thuế, phòng 
chờ thương gia và tối ưu hoạt động tại các điểm kinh doanh đ ược kỳ vọng tác động 
tích c ực lên biên l ợi nhu ận của AST . Chúng tôi dự phóng biên l ợi nhu ận g ộp đ ạt 
64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027, hỗ trợ LNST công ty mẹ tăng lần lượt 48%/17% 
yoy.

không còn ghi nhận doanh thu 1 lần 
từ phí nước thải; trong khi LNST

thúc đẩy nhờ thời tiết nắng nóng hơn; 
trong khi tri ển v ọng nhóm khách 
hàng công nghi ệp b ị ảnh hư ởng b ởi 
bất lợi thuế quan

lý rác thải được phê duyệt cho phép 
cải thiện doanh thu và lợi nhuận của 
mảng này

Khuyến nghị MUA với giá mục tiêu 
59,000 VNĐ/cổ phiếu

Doanh thu 2Q2026 đạt 1,222 tỷ (-8% YoY/18.1% QoQ) và LNST công ty mẹ đạt 325 tỷ 
VND (7% YoY/55% QoQ), ghi nhận tăng trưởng của tất cả các mảng. Mảng cung cấp 
nước tăng trưởng ổn định, tiếp tục là trụ cột chính của BWE, đóng góp doanh thu 785 
tỷ VND (+12% YoY/12% QoQ) nhờ nhà máy nước Long An v ận hành từ Q3/2025 giúp 
sản lượng nước tăng 28% YoY.

Cường độ và thời gian kéo dài c ủa các đ ợt nắng nóng trong 2026/2027 là cơ s ở để 
chúng tôi đi ều chỉnh tăng tăng trư ởng tiêu th ụ nước của nhóm khách hàng dân cư 
tại Bình Dương và Bình Phư ớc trong năm 2026 - 2027 lên mức 7.5/6.5% YoY. Trái lại, 
triển vọng tăng trưởng tiêu thụ nước nhóm sản xuất ảm đạm do tình hình thuế quan 
có diễn biến bất lợi trong ngắn hạn cho các ngành xu ất khẩu truyền thống của Việt 
Nam.

UBND TP .HCM công bố đơn giá thu gom, vận chuyển và xử lý rác thải mới, tăng mạnh 
từ 50-150% nhằm phản ánh đầy đủ và chính xác hơn các chi phí phát sinh trong thực 
tế. Chúng tôi cho rằng điều này sẽ tác động tích cực với BWE khi các dự án của doanh

Ngưỡng kỹ thuật

VNINDEX điều chỉnh trở lại trong tuần qua sau khi gặp thử thách tại vùng cản quanh 1850, 
hình thành cây nến tuần giảm điểm nhưng với phần bóng dưới khá dài cho thấy sự tham 
gia tích cực của lực cầu bắt đáy tại vùng hỗ trợ gần. Trên khung ngày, nhịp giảm mạnh giữa 
tuần đã khiến chỉ số đánh mất đáy ngắn hạn tuần trước và chớm cắt xuống dưới SMA200, 
trong khi phiên hồi phục cuối tuần chưa cho thấy sự cải thiện rõ rệt của lực cầu. Mặc dù 
diễn biến này cho thấy áp lực điều chỉnh vẫn có thể còn tiếp diễn, tuy nhiên khi xu hướng 
tăng ngắn hạn chưa bị phá vỡ, VNINDEX được kỳ vọng sẽ nhận được lực đỡ tại vùng hỗ trợ 
gần quanh 1750, tương ứng vùng hợp lưu quanh SMA50, và có cơ hội hồi phục trở lại.

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Moderate Growth, Broadening Opportunities
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Although the Citi Economic Surprise Index has been easing over the past month as a
rising number of data releases have fallen short of elevated expectations (Exhibit 1), the
data remains broadly positive and consistent with sustained moderate growth. Indeed,
with alternating waves of headwinds and tailwinds, real consumer spending and gross

domestic product (GDP) continue to expand near their longer-run average rates even
with substantial wealth effects and strong growth in government transfer payments.
Employment and hours worked have barely increased over the past year, leaving
productivity to account for nearly all the economic growth. Combined with moderating
wage growth, this has contained unit labor costs—a positive for inflation dynamics.
Despite stubborn readings above the Federal Reserve's (Fed) 2% target inflation through

July, consumer price index data were relatively favorable, and although the latest personal
consumption expenditures (PCE) report was less reassuring, much of the upside pressure
remained concentrated in healthcare, tariff- and energy-sensitive categories, as well as
in volatile financial services that tend to follow equity-market gains. The latter inflation
category is slated to be revised lower when new index methodologies are announced
next month. Meanwhile, a broad range of other goods and services showed signs of

cooling inflation on a three-month annualized basis, as shown in Exhibit 2.
Exhibit 1: Economic Data Surprises Have Been More Mixed Over The Past Month…

Source: Bloomberg. Data as of August 26, 2026. It is not possible to invest directly in an index. Please refer to index
definitions at the end of this report. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

Basically, tariffs, elevated oil prices, wealth effects and AI-related electronics price
inflation continue to put upward pressure on inflation and interest rates. Although higher
gasoline prices have largely offset the One Big Beautiful Bill Act’s (OBBBA) direct benefit
to consumers, the shift from a negative fiscal impulse in 2025 to a positive impulse this
year has supported aggregate demand, contributing to the stalled progress on headline
inflation. Government transfers increased rapidly in July to a new high as a share of

personal income, as aging baby boomers retire.
Upside pressures on inflation have been counteracted in large part by slowing wage
growth, subdued housing activity, relatively tame credit growth, strong productivity, and
soft unit labor cost increases. In our view, if demand materially exceeded productive
capacity, inflation reacceleration would be much more pervasive.
Also important, the effective tariff rate is expected to average approximately 10%

this year, broadly matching its 2025 average absent another policy shock. The tariff
contribution to inflation is thus expected to continue to fade from its estimated
early-2026 peak of roughly 0.8 percentage point. Further oil-price moderation and
methodological changes affecting financial-service prices and other categories seen
as misrepresenting true inflation will also help improve the picture. Alongside anchored
longer-term inflation expectations, this suggests a gradual further moderation in

inflation, which would improve household purchasing power and support economic

### Page 3

3 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
growth. Real consumer spending appears on track for approximately 2.5% to 3% annualized
growth in Q3, following + 0.5% in Q1 and +3.5% in Q2.
Also positive, business investment in equipment and intellectual property accelerated strongly
in the first half. Business investment in manufactured goods is particularly positive for
broadening economic growth given manufacturing activity’s high multiplier effect on jobs and

aggregate demand. Helped by surging AI investment, OBBBA tax incentives, and rebounding
profitability, manufacturing activity is rapidly improving after several weak years, helping
sustain real GDP growth near 2.1% year-over-year, in line with its 25-year average. Although
July “core” capital-goods orders were disappointing, June was revised substantially higher,
raising estimates for Q3 business investment. Strong regional manufacturing surveys also

point to strong momentum, mostly the direct result of surging AI-related investment.
While there is no single accepted estimate for AI investment in the U.S., it is generally
projected at around $600 billion in 2026, $850 billion in 2027, $950 billion in 2028, and over
$1 trillion in 2029. While reaching new highs, its growth rate—and therefore its contribution
to GDP growth—is eventually projected to moderate from +50% to +60% in 2026 and 2027
to +10% by 2029. Also important, its domestic impact is smaller than the headline total

suggests. According to industry estimates, 70% of AI spending is directed toward equipment,
most of it imported. Thus, only around 50% of projected U.S. AI investment is poised to
directly help the domestic economy—though still enough to help broaden growth through
expanding domestic data-center construction, equipment production, power generation, and
supporting infrastructure.
The manufacturing sector’s broader spillovers to suppliers, construction, transportation, and

services make the sector’s recovery an important support for continued expansion. Consistent
with surging AI capital expenditures, rebounding industrial production, and very strong profit
estimates both here and abroad, BofA’s Global Wave 1 indicator has continued to increase—a
typically favorable indicator for cyclical assets’ performance relative to defensives.
With the growth and inflation outlook remaining positive, concerns have shifted to fiscal

strains and uncertainty, as the 2026 deficit appears likely to exceed the Congressional Budget
Office’s February projection by about $200 billion, and publicly-held debt is projected to
rise from approximately 101% of GDP in 2026 to 120% by 2036—a path that could test
investor confidence.
However, since there is no identifiable debt threshold that automatically triggers a crisis,
and comparisons with smaller or externally indebted economies are imperfect, the loss of

confidence in the sustainability of the debt has been short-lived. Indeed, U.S. debt is dollar-
denominated and approximately 70% is domestically held. Deep capital markets and the dollar’s
global reserve currency role sustain structural Treasury demand. Also important, healthy private
sector balance sheets and productivity growth leave scope for gradual fiscal consolidation in
a solidly growing economy without the dire negative effects on the economy observed in past

debt crises overseas. Thus, despite recent concerns, Treasury yields remain within their three-
year range and broadly consistent with moderate growth and anchored inflation expectations,
their recent variations largely tracking energy prices and geopolitical developments.
Also important, the term premium has returned to pre-global-financial-crisis levels following
exceptionally low levels in the post-2008-2009 decade. However, its increase appears to

be just a normalization in a delayed response to previous monetary tightening. Indeed, the
estimated 10-year term premium—or compensation required by investors to hold long-term
debt rather than roll over short-term debt—has historically followed the federal funds rate
with a substantial lag of almost 3 years.
While government debt supply, central-bank balance sheet policy, inflation, and interest-rate
volatility shape the term premium, the lagged fed funds–term premium correlation suggests

that the 2024 to 2025 policy easing is likely to act as a moderating force on the term premium
for the foreseeable future. In our view, the growth and interest rate environment is, therefore,
likely to remain constructive for continued expansion and cyclical assets’ performance,
including materials and infrastructure investments while fiscal uncertainty, soft labor demand,
and an eventually moderating AI investment impulse argue for portfolio diversification.

1 BofA Global Wave quantifies global economic trends, and it is used to forecast stock market performance.

### Page 4

4 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Taking Stock of the End of Capital Abundance
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
For much of the past quarter century, the global economy enjoyed a remarkable luxury:
Capital was abundant, cheap and seemingly limitless.
Savings flowed freely across borders. Global interest rates drifted steadily lower and,
for a time, actually turned negative across parts of Europe and Japan. Governments

borrowed with few apparent consequences. Corporations financed growth at historically
attractive rates. Households gained access to inexpensive credit. And investors prospered
as declining discount rates helped push the valuations of stocks, bonds, real estate and
other financial assets higher.
Few countries benefited more from this age of capital abundance than the U.S. As the
world's largest debtor nation and home to the world's deepest capital markets, America

tapped the global savings pool with extraordinary ease. Foreign capital helped finance
persistent federal budget and current account deficits while keeping U.S. borrowing costs
remarkably low. Washington could spend more than it collected, households could consume
more than they earned, and the country as a whole could invest more than it saved.
For decades, the world was happy to finance the difference. That comfortable
arrangement is now being challenged. At precisely the moment when America's financing

needs have rarely been greater, the age of abundant capital is giving way to something
very different: The age of capital competition.
Importantly, the world is not running out of savings. According to the International
Monetary Fund, gross global savings remain around 27% of world GDP , roughly in line
with the average of this century. The supply of savings has not collapsed.
What has changed is the other side of the equation. The world has developed an

enormous appetite for capital. Governments, corporations and households are increasingly
competing for essentially the same pool of savings. And when demand for something
rises faster than its supply, its price tends to rise. For capital, that price is the interest rate.
The past was different. The previous era of abundance was unusual not merely because
the world saved a great deal but because investment demand was comparatively weak.
China, Germany, Japan, oil-producing nations and other surplus economies generated

enormous pools of excess savings. Emerging market central banks accumulated foreign
exchange reserves, much of which found its way into U.S. Treasury securities. Meanwhile,
following the global financial crisis, private investment across much of the developed
world remained subdued.
The upshot: Too much capital was chasing too few attractive and potential investment
opportunities.
Today, that equation is reversing. Global savings remain plentiful, but the list of claimants

on that capital is exploding. We call them the Five Ds: Debt, Defense, Digitalization,
Demographics and Deglobalization.
Start with debt. Governments across the developed world are running large structural
deficits while simultaneously confronting rising interest expenses. The U.S. stands
out. Federal debt held by the public is approaching the size of annual U.S. GDP , and
Washington must finance large new deficits while continually refinancing trillions of

dollars of existing obligations (Exhibits 3 and 4). Treasurys are therefore competing for
capital on a massive scale.
Then comes defense. Russia's invasion of Ukraine, tensions in the Middle East and
strategic competition between the U.S. and China have ended the post-Cold War peace
dividend. America is rebuilding munitions inventories, Europe is rearming, and Asian
Investment Implications
The age of capital competition will likely
favor quality companies with strong

balance sheets, durable cash flows and
capital discipline, in our view. Investors
should consider from the global
capital spending cycle via exposure to
themes such as power and electrical
infrastructure, defense, robotics,
semiconductors and data centers.

### Page 5

5 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
nations are increasing military expenditures. Ships, missiles, satellites, drones and
defense-production facilities all require capital.
Third is digitalization. The AI revolution may prove to be among the most capital-
intensive technological transformations in history. Technology companies are spending
extraordinary sums on data centers, advanced semiconductors, networking equipment

and power generation. Utilities need capital to expand and modernize electrical grids.
Semiconductor manufacturers require tens of billions of dollars to build advanced
fabrication plants. All the above is enormously expensive and demands capital.
Fourth is demographics. Aging populations are placing growing pressure on
government budgets through pensions, healthcare and other social programs. Fewer
workers relative to retirees mean greater fiscal burdens across much of the developed

world. In the U.S. alone, mandatory spending outlays on Medicaid, Medicare and Social
Security have jumped from $724 billion in 2000 to $3.2 trillion in FY 2025.
Finally, there is deglobalization. For decades, globalization was fundamentally about
efficiency: Produce goods wherever costs were lowest. Today's world increasingly prizes
resilience and national security, which means the duplication of supply chains, reshored
factories, expanding strategic inventories and subsidized domestic manufacturing

facilities. All these objectives, of course, require capital.
Indeed, the common denominator of debt, defense, digitalization, demographics and
deglobalization is access to capital. And since these factors are structural rather than
cyclical, their demands on global savings are unlikely to fade any time soon.
Evidence of this emerging competition is increasingly visible in financial markets.
Investment-grade companies have already sold nearly $1.5 trillion of bonds this year,

roughly 36% more than during the comparable period last year and on pace to challenge
previous records, according to Bloomberg. With hyperscalers expected to continue
pouring capital into AI infrastructure through the end of the decade, corporate demand
for financing could remain elevated for years.
Meanwhile, the U.S. Treasury must finance enormous federal deficits. The result is an
increasingly crowded marketplace in which governments and corporations are competing

for the same investor dollars. Recent shifts by investment grade bond funds away from
Treasurys and toward corporate debt offer a small but telling glimpse of what this new
environment may look like (Exhibits 5 and 6).
For investors, the implications are significant. First, real interest rates may remain
structurally higher than during much of the previous two decades. Investors waiting for a
return to the ultra-low-rate environment of the 2010s may be waiting a very long time.

Second, fiscal policy matters again. In an era of abundant capital, markets often tolerated
fiscal excess. In a world of greater capital competition, countries that borrow heavily
without generating corresponding economic returns may eventually pay a higher price.
Keep a close eye on Japan.
Third, capital allocation matters more. Companies capable of generating strong returns
on invested capital, maintaining healthy balance sheets and funding expansion internally

should command a premium. When money is no longer virtually free, the difference
between productive investment and financial engineering becomes much more important.
Fourth, the world's enormous capital spending cycle should create winners. Power
generation, electrical infrastructure, defense, industrial automation, semiconductors, data
centers and other capital-intensive industries could enjoy years of elevated investment
and higher-than-expected earnings growth, in our view.

In the end, the age of capital abundance is fading—the era whereby capital basically
chased borrowers is past. Prologue will be defined by borrowers chasing capital. The tide
has turned.

6 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What portfolio addition could potentially help mitigate a sharp rise in inflation and/or yields?
How about the start of a new hiking cycle where short-term interest rates rise consistently?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
• If inflation or a weaker U.S. dollar are the reason for a sharp rise in yields, then
increasing exposure to Commodities as a complement to a core portfolio makes sense.

Above-average inflation that is consistent and not excessive mixed with a weaker
U.S. dollar are generally tailwinds for commodities and the companies that mine for
them. Additionally, if global economic growth is running above trend and the U.S. dollar
weakens as it normalizes relative to other currencies, then adding non-U.S Developed
Markets and Emerging Markets (EM) exposure also makes sense.
• If short rates rise sharply due to a new interest rate hiking cycle, a more defensive tilt

in asset exposure makes sense. In other words, in the equity markets more defensive
sectors become more in demand, lower-volatility and higher-quality areas become
more in demand, cyclical shares tend to feel downward pressure, and highly levered
areas without a ton of debt are generally impacted the most. If the short-term rate
rise is minimal and a new hiking cycle is not beginning, then a solid mixture of Value
and Growth Equities, laddered portfolio in bonds, exposure to commodities and

thematic ideas make the most sense overall, in our view.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Personal Consumption Expenditures Price Index is a key economic measure that tracks the average change in prices paid by U.S. consumers for goods and services.
Citi Economic Surprise Index is an economic indicator that measures whether published macroeconomic data is beating or missing market expectations.
Consumer Price Index is a measure of the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative market basket of consumer goods and services.

conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
De-reservification, Not De-dollarization
The world isn’t moving away from the dollar. It is shifting its dollars from traditional
reserves to state banks, pension funds, and other quasi-sovereign investors.
(Bernardo Montoya/Courtesy Reuters)
PUBLISHED
September 15, 2026 8:37 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
This is a joint blog with Alex Etra, Senior Macro
Strategist and head of Flow of Funds at Vanda
Research.
The IMF’s data on the currency composition of
foreign exchange (FX) reserves is scrutinized carefully
for any signs that the world is shifting way from the
dollar.
Yet it doesn’t really matter that much if the dollar’s
share of reserves has shifted from 56.5 percent to 57
percent. The ink-to-impact ratio of the quarterly

reporting on the latest COFER data is all off.
Neither the stock of global reserves nor the stock of
dollar reserves has changed much in the last ten years.
The action is elsewhere.

### Page 3

3
China’s state banks (per the Bank of International
Settlements) have almost as many foreign assets as the
central bank (PBOC).
Japan’s Government Pension Investment Fund
(GPIF) has almost as many foreign assets ($986
billion) as the government has FX reserves ($1.1
trillion at the end of August). Japan’s FX reserves are
on the books of the Ministry of Finance and the
GPIF is overseen by the Ministry of Health, Welfare
and Labor—so these are almost all assets of the

Government of Japan or the broader public sector,
not its central bank.
Korea’s National Pension Service has more foreign
assets than the Bank of Korea has FX reserves.
A different dynamic is in play in Taiwan, as the
government doesn’t run a big retirement fund. But in

### Page 4

4
a world where the hedge ratio of the life insurers has
been (and still is) used as a policy tool, the shift in
Taiwan’s foreign asset accumulation away from the
Central Bank of China (CBC) and over to
institutions whose activities in the foreign currency
market it directly influences broadly fits into the same
theme. So too does the PBOC’s use of the
“macroprudential adjustment factor” effectively adjust
banks’ net foreign assets in much the same way that

CBC regulations adjust lifer hedge ratios.
In the last year, the lifers’ hedge ratios—and their
direct hedges with the central bank—have been one
of the key tools the CBC has used to manage the
Taiwan dollar (and guard the lifers’ solvency).
Asia’s big surplus economies are in a sense just
catching up with the world’s oil exporters. Norway
put its oil surplus into a sovereign wealth fund from
the start. Kuwait, Qatar, and Abu Dhabi (the most
oil-rich emirate) all also have large sovereign funds.

And the world’s biggest oil exporter has joined them:
The Public Investment Fund has almost as many
foreign assets as the Saudi Central Bank (SAMA).*
What’s more, the available evidence suggests that the
dollar share of these pools is in line with or higher
than the dollar share of global FX reserves (it depends
on the institution and jurisdiction).

### Page 5

5
In other words, by looking at the (easily available)
data on the world’s static holdings of formal FX
reserve assets, scholars and analysts miss most of the
growth in the world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign
foreign assets.
Consider China.
No serious analyst now disputes that China’s state
banks—including the policy banks (the China
Development Bank, China Exim)—hold several
trillion in foreign assets.
China reports $3.3 trillion in gross foreign assets to

the BIS (the net position, counting foreign bank
claims on the entire Chinese economy not just the
banks, is $2.5 trillion). That maps to the BOP data,
which shows almost $4 trillion in gross outflows
through the banking system (technically, the sum of
gross outflows in “other” plus the $500 billion in
foreign currency bonds held by the state commercial
banks)

### Page 6

6
Since 2010 (and even more so after 2014) the net
outflow through the state banks has exceeded reserve
accumulation.
The broad contours of this story are confirmed by the
balance sheet data reported by state commercial
banks in their 2025 annual reports, which showed

### Page 7

7
that the top five banks held a combined $2.5 trillion
in foreign currency assets (mostly held abroad).
The Chinese haven’t disclosed the foreign assets of
the two policy banks (with at this stage the complicity
of the IMF, which has neither analyzed the role of
SAFE policy bank financing, nor highlighted the
glaring gap in China’s own reporting). But the work
of AidData points to nearly $1 trillion in foreign
assets, with a hefty dollar share.
The available data sources all suggest that the bulk of

banks—puts the dollar share of their offshore foreign
currency assets at around 70 percent. That is well
above the last disclosed dollar share of China’s formal
reserves (55 percent in 2019). The banks’ annual

### Page 8

8
reports suggest that about 70 percent of the banks’
assets are in dollars and Hong Kong dollars (55
percent USD/15 percent HKD)—a bit below the
dollar and Hong Kong dollar share of their liabilities
(swaps out of the HKD fund a decent share of the
banks euro assets).
Put simply, SAFE’s static dollar holdings aren’t the
important story.
The real story is the rapid growth of the state banks
dollar holdings.
Japan holds a high (though undisclosed) dollar share

in its FX reserves, and those reserves are primarily
invested in Treasuries—so Japan’s MoF is now clearly
the largest contributor to the U.S. data on foreign
official holdings of Treasuries. (SAFE has shifted its
funds out of U.S. custodians, and thus increasingly
appears in the data as a “private” holder in a European
custodial center)
Japan’s formal reserves haven’t changed much in the
last ten years; periodic sales to limit the yen’s weakness
have offset most of the MoF’s accumulated interest

income. Foreign currency reserves were $1.2 trillion
in 2014 and $1.16 trillion at the end of Q1. They
dipped to about $1 trillion at the end of August after
sales of close to $100 billion at the end of July/start of
August.

### Page 9

9
What has grown is the foreign portfolio of the GPIF.
it was around $400 billion in 2014, ~$1 trillion in Q1
of 2026.
GPIF’s foreign bonds aren’t held exclusively in dollars,
but roughly 52 percent of its foreign bonds (26
percent of all bonds) are USD-denominated.
But the GPIF, like most international investors, holds
a large share of its equity portfolio in the U.S. (~$165
billion, 65 percent of the foreign equity portfolio).
Korea is a more extreme version of Japan.

The Bank of Korea’s FX reserves have been roughly
$375-450 billion over the last ten years or so. Around
two-thirds of those are now in dollars (as of the end of
2025).

### Page 10

10
From 2012 to 2025, the foreign assets at the NPS
have grown from ~$80 billion (around 21 percent of
total assets) to over $660 billion (55 percent of total
assets)—and something like 70 percent of those are in
dollars. 76 percent of the fund’s equities are in dollars,
and 66 percent of its debt. And while a lower share of
its “alternative” portfolio is in North America, most
alternative mandates are awarded in dollars.

### Page 11

11
In other words, the majority of the dollar assets held
by Korea’s government (the NPS reports to the
Ministry of Health and Welfare, and the Minister of
Health chairs its asset allocation committee) aren’t
held by the central bank and aren’t in the IMF’s
COFER data set.
Taiwan is an unusual case, as the life insurers aren’t
technically sovereign investors. But with their
regulator now explicitly encouraging the them to hold
an unhedged foreign currency book and hence they

now rely on the state for their solvency, they have a bit
of an official character (a significant appreciation of
the TWD would blow through their volatility reserves
and leave them with fewer assets than liabilities, as
their foreign assets are largely held against TWD
policies). The CBC certainly now uses the lifers desire
to unwind existing hedges to help manage the Taiwan

### Page 12

12
dollar. And they have an exceptionally high dollar
share in their foreign assets—over 95 percent.
The Saudi Public Investment Fund (PIF) is another
example.
It keeps roughly $200 billion of its $250 billion in
foreign investments in dollars—well above the U.S.
share of a standard global stock index. Almost all of
the PIF’s $30 billion in external debt is also in dollars.
And the PIF now has only slightly fewer foreign
assets than the Saudi Central Bank has reserves.**

The broad story is thus pretty clear: the growth in the
world’s sovereign and quasi-sovereign assets is not
coming through an increase in FX reserve holdings
managed by the world’s central banks.

### Page 13

13
And, in the critical case of China, the dollar share of
China’s quasi-sovereign foreign assets is likely higher
than the dollar share of their formal FX reserves.
Adam Tooze has noted that inflows into the U.S. are
now coming from investors looking for a profit, not
investors looking for safety. What he calls the “profit
dollar” manifests itself in a number of different ways.
More inflows into corporate bonds and equities for
one. Less official demand for Treasuries for another.

More opacity too.
Those same trends are at play among sovereign and
quasi-sovereign investors.
So don’t obsess about the dollar’s chare in formal FX
reserves. Do recognize that the dollar’s “reserve
currency role” isn’t the source of any significant new
inflows into the dollar.

### Page 14

14
The dollar’s dominant role in the international
monetary system depends on much more than the
size and composition of central bank FX reserves. It is
as much, perhaps more so (given that FX reserve
managers are themselves ultimately liability matchers)
a function of the portfolio choice of a set of
private/semi-private/quasi-sovereign investors that
are much more difficult to observe. Reserve currency
status is not only about FX reserves in a strict sense.

That’s been true for some time. Most of the current
flow into U.S. from the “official” sector is coming
from investors who are not classic reserve managers.
Those flows remain heavily tilted toward the dollar, at
least for now.***
And any real de-dollarization would likely occur first
among these investors.
Put differently, the dollar’s global role is increasingly
as a source of returns, not a source of safety. The
foreign bid, private and public, is for risk, not for

Treasuries. That doesn’t help Scott Bessent much
right now, but it has helped keep valuations in the
stock market extended. And the global debate on the
dollar’s role lags the evolution in the dollar’s role, and
the risks associated with that new role.
It is currently fashionable in some circles to point to
the diminution of the “convenience yield” on

### Page 15

15
Treasuries even while the U.S. dollar continues to
enjoy such a privilege. But the shift in the global
investor base, and their apparent portfolio preference
for U.S. risk assets in lieu of Treasuries, may help
explain the divergence between the convenience yield
on UST and the convenience yield on USD.
And of course, if the negative convenience yield
implies that Treasuries are trading at a historical
discount, the persistence of the latter—and its

concentration in risk assets—suggests that the “profit
dollar” itself is likely trading at a historical premium.
That apparent premium on U.S. risk assets deserves as
much (or more) scrutiny as the currency composition
of central bank FX reserves.
* The PIF and Saudi Aramco also now have a certain
amount of debt.
** Reserves (now mostly in Treasuries, so should
appear in the TIC data, unlike at some times in the
past.

### Page 16

16
This work represents the views solely of the author(s). The
Council on Foreign Relations is an independent,
nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and
publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters
of policy.

share is below the likely dollar share of Japan’s MoF.

DeepSeek 计划领投机器⼈ AI 初创公司 Winverse AI 数千万美元种⼦轮融资，交易完成后该
公司估值预计达 4 亿美元。继投资宇树科技后， DeepSeek 进⼀步布局具⾝智能。 Winverse
由前腾讯科学家胡瀚创⽴，专注开发能够理解指令、处理现实任务的机器⼈ AI 模型，瞄准
⼈形机器⼈产业的软件技术瓶颈。
摘要：
DeepSeek 正将业务触⻆延伸⾄⼈形机器⼈领域。
10 ⽉ 12 ⽇，据彭博报道， DeepSeek 已同意领投 Winverse AI 的种⼦轮融资，⾦额达数千万美
元。报道援引知情⼈⼠透露，这笔交易将使这家成⽴仅数⽉的初创公司估值达到约 4 亿美元。美
团旗下⻰珠投资（ Long-Z Investments ）等机构也已作出投资承诺。

这将成为 DeepSeek 迄今对外部初创企业最⼤规模的投资之⼀。此前， DeepSeek 已投资宇树科
技。 2026 年 8 ⽉， DeepSeek 斥资约 1.41 亿元⼈⺠币参与宇树科技 IPO ，进⼀步布局具⾝智能领
域。 DeepSeek 最新⼀轮融资规模接近 800 亿元⼈⺠币，超过原定⽬标，部分资⾦预计将⽤于与
外部初创企业联合开发。
不过，上述交易尚未最终敲定，估值及投资⽅参与情况仍可能发⽣变化。
前腾讯科学家创⽴，专攻机器⼈ AI
Winverse AI 由胡瀚于 2026 年创⽴。胡瀚此前在腾讯担任视觉语⾔与具⾝基础模型团队负责⼈，
拥有清华⼤学博⼠学位，曾任职于微软亚洲研究院和百度。
Winverse AI 专注于为机器⼈开发 AI 模型，⽬标是让机器⼈能够理解语⾳指令、处理现实世界中
的复杂任务，⽽不仅是执⾏预设动作或完成运动控制。这类技术需要针对机器⼈应⽤场景进⾏
专⻔训练，与 ChatGPT 、 DeepSeek 等主要⾯向⽂本交互的语⾔模型有所不同。
中国在⼈形机器⼈硬件制造领域已具备全球领先优势，宇树科技今年 8 ⽉完成 9 亿美元上市，进

⼀步凸显了这⼀领域的资本关注度。不过，如何赋予机器⼈理解环境、处理复杂任务的智能能
⼒，仍是⾏业⾯临的主要挑战。

Document 234: EMEA FX Talking_ External headwinds up against local stories

Source PDF: all report pdf/EMEA FX Talking_ External headwinds up against local stories.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

Like many emerging market currencies, those in the EMEA
region have been hit by higher US Treasury yields and the
stronger dollar. Yet the domestic stories look constructive for
many, and a combination of some local rate hikes as well as
light foreign positioning (ex Hungary) can insulate these
currencies from the global bond market sell-oﬀ

Rafal Benecki, Peter Virovacz, David Havrlant, Mateusz Sutowicz,
Dmitry Dolgin, Muhammet Mercan and Chris Turner
Light foreign positioning and some local rate hikes can keep EMEA currencies
relatively insulated
EUR/PLN: Zloty swings with market sentiment
  Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 12M
EUR/PLN 4.3877 Mildly Bearish 4.36 4.35 4.33 4.29
EUR/PLN has moved towards 4.40 after trading around the 4.30
level during summer. With market sentiment deteriorating amid
geopolitical tensions in Iran, France and Russia, the coming weeks
could challenge the zloty.

PLN has recently strengthened its correlation with EUR/USD, so the
dollar’s future moves will be the key driver in the currency’s
performance, while domestic factors are likely to take a back seat.
We retain a broadly positive stance on the zloty in the medium
term due to solid GDP momentum and persistent outperformance.
However, the balance of risks has become less favourable; the
National Bank of Poland may deliver fewer hikes than priced, and a

hot budget debate could reveal vulnerabilities.

EUR/HUF: A credibility boost is in the making for
the forint
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 1
EUR/HUF 364.89 Bearish 360.00 355.00 360.00 36
While EUR/HUF has shown some volatility recently, the swings have
been far more moderate than we used to see. This reﬂects general
positioning and the euro adoption story.
We think an idiosyncratic shock is coming with the 2027 budget

and the mid-term ﬁscal consolidation plan. If the government
delivers a credible plan, as we expect, and announces oﬃcial talks
on joining ERM II, this could provide a credibility boost to the forint.
A signiﬁcant drop in the risk premium and some insulation from
geopolitical market turbulence could take EUR/HUF down to the
350-355 area over the next month. This would, however, reopen
the door to a National Bank of Hungary easing cycle starting in

December, putting the HUF on a mild depreciation path over the
following 12 months.

EUR/CZK: Braving the turbulence
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 12M
EUR/CZK 24.39 Neutral 24.40 24.40 24.35 24.30
The Czech Republic has also been aﬀected by the global bond yield
rally, with the turmoil applying depreciation pressure on the
koruna. Inﬂation is set to increase on the back of rallying import
prices and second-round eﬀects of the oil price shock starting to
bite.
We believe that equilibrium interest rates are higher than generally

assumed, since the cost of capital must adjust to a world of
protracted conﬂict and increased resource scarcity. A higher base
rate is warranted to prevent the koruna from weakening further,
which would exacerbate domestic price pressures.
That said, the ECB may turn more hesitant despite elevated
inﬂation, as concerns over French ﬁscal issues rekindle fears of
another euro crisis. In turn, the real interest rate diﬀerential should
help the koruna withstand the turmoil and gradually appreciate

once things have calmed down.

EUR/RON: Rating downgrade avoided, RON still
vulnerable
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 12M
EUR/RON 5.3383 Neutral 5.35 5.35 5.40 5.45
EUR/RON had its second major move of the year, climbing from
5.28 to 5.35, which looks to be the near-term level. Oﬃcial
interventions have likely moderated the impact of signiﬁcant
outﬂow pressures through both September and so far in October –
with the fear of rating downgrades and the protracted political

instability as key drivers.
While the rating remained in place, the political arena is yet to ﬁnd
its consensus. The key positive element supporting the credit story
remains the budget deﬁcit, which stood at 2.89% of GDP as of
August 2026 (vs 4.50% in August last year).
A tighter liquidity management has recently led FX implied yields
to the highest levels since May 2025, although funding pressures
have diminished from their early October peak. We expect the pair

to remain around its current levels by year-end and close next year
near 5.45.

EUR/RSD: RSD stable ahead of parliamentary
elections
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 1
EUR/RSD 117.42 Neutral 117.40 117.40 117.30 11
EUR/RSD has remained relatively ﬂat in the range of 117.3–117.5.
Yet again, the US Treasury's Oﬃce of Foreign Assets Control (OFAC)
has extended the operating waiver for Serbia's NIS reﬁnery until 30
October. The NIS ownership issue is yet to be resolved.
Snap parliamentary elections have been called for 25 October, with

from 3.0% to 3.5% of GDP in 2026, mainly reﬂecting higher social
expenditure.

rate in place at 5.75%, continuing to highlight inﬂation uncertainty.
Between January and August, the Bank sold €407m to keep the
pair stable. We expect the pair to remain around the current levels

ahead.

USD/UAH: NBU efforts to protect the UAH
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 12M
USD/UAH 44.92 Neutral 45.00 45.20 45.30 45.50
The hryvnia remained under pressure as the dollar strengthened,
partly due to renewed Middle East tensions. Consequently,
USD/UAH temporarily broke above 45.0, reaching its highest level
on record.
Hryvnia depreciation could have been sharper, but National Bank of
Ukraine action provided support. In September, the central bank

raised rates by 50bp to 16.0% and maintained sizeable FX
interventions, with the four-week average near record highs.
The outlook for the hryvnia appears cloudy as the macroeconomic
environment remains challenging – particularly as the ongoing war
with Russia poses an increasing threat to the Ukrainian economy.

USD/KZT: Growing state FX sales backlog to
boost KZT resilience
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 1
USD/KZT 452.04 Neutral 455.00 450.00 440.00 46
The tenge gained 5% against the US dollar in September, driven
mainly by private capital inﬂows, particularly continued non-
resident demand for KZT government bonds.
Combined net FX sales by the state oil fund (NFRK) and National
Bank of Kazakhstan fell to zero in September, compared with $1.0–

1.3bn per month in 1H26. As NFRK sales have remained below ﬁscal
ﬁnancing needs since mid-2026, an estimated $1.0–1.5bn backlog
of FX sales has accumulated.
After gaining 25% YoY against USD, the tenge has limited upside,
particularly amid global risk aversion and regional geopolitical
uncertainty. At the same time, Kazakhstan’s high real rates should
continue to attract carry-trade inﬂows, while the eventual release
of the state FX sales backlog could ensure KZT resilience if market

sentiment deteriorates.

USD/UZS: Still waiting for gold exports to catch
up
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6
USD/UZS 11898.00 Bearish 11650.00 11500.00 1160
After appreciating 1% against the US dollar in August, the soum
was broadly ﬂat in September and early October. This was slightly
weaker than we expected, but still resilient given the global dollar
rally and lower gold prices.
Gold exports remain a key variable for Uzbekistan’s FX market.

There were no exports in August, and gold was exported only in
April and July this year. Year-to-date volumes stand at just 18 mt,
implying a substantial backlog compared with 85 mt in FY25 and
the historical annual range of 80–120 mt.
Like Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan oﬀers high real rates and is likely to
continue doing so despite a potential monetary policy easing. As
inﬂation has stabilised at 6.2–6.4% YoY, we see some room for a
cautious cut from the current 14.00% policy rate before the end of

the year.

USD/TRY: Rate cut expectations build as
inﬂation eases
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 12M
USD/TRY 49.34 Bullish 49.75 51.50 54.30 59.80
Fund volatility has not aﬀected the dollarisation ratio. Net outﬂows
in lira-denominated funds turned TRY625bn between the end of
August and 6 October, excluding suspended funds. Meanwhile,
outﬂows from FX-linked fund assets totalled $2.2bn, while higher
TRY deposits and repo balances suggest dollarisation remains

contained.
September’s CPI came in below consensus, supported by
favourable food and transport. Annual CPI fell to 29.7% from 31.5%
in August. Seasonally adjusted CPI, a key indicator closely
monitored by the Central Bank of Turkey, slowed to 1.6% month-
on-month from 2.2% in August, marking one of the lowest readings
seen in recent years.
This backdrop increases the likelihood of a shift toward policy rate
cuts. The recent investment fund disruption is likely to raise

downside risks to activity. Given September’s CPI and weak
domestic demand, we expect two 100bp cuts in October and
December.

USD/ZAR: A resilient performance from the rand
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 1
USD/ZAR 16.51 Mildly Bearish 16.50 16.50 16.25 16
The high-yield rand has been one of the better EM performers
during the sell-oﬀ over the last month. Its ﬁscal metrics are not
particularly great, (5% budget deﬁcit, 80% debt to GDP), but the
coalition government looks to be taking things in the right
direction; a primary budget surplus is helpful here.

The South African Reserve Bank is helping out too, having hiked
25bp in September to 7.25%. It is threatening to hike rates again
on a baseline view that core central banks hike another 50bp.
A diﬃcult external environment suggests USD/ZAR may need to
trade in a 16.00-16.60 range for the next three to six months. As
2027 progresses, however, the case for sub-16.00 levels builds.

USD/ILS: Israel’s higher credit rating provides a
buffer
Spot One month bias 1M 3M 6M 12M
USD/ILS 3.0713 Neutral 3.05 3.05 3.05 3.05
Israel’s high-quality credit rating (A-) has provided a buﬀer to the
shekel during this debt crisis. That largely stems from Israel’s strong
external investment position and relatively large domestic capital
market. Additionally, the debt sell-oﬀ has yet to unhinge the US
tech story – of which the shekel is a beneﬁciary.

In September, the Bank of Israel cut rates to 3.25%, citing low
inﬂation. Markets currently price further aggressive cuts to the
2.00% area, which seems excessive. The BoI might prefer to save
those rate cuts for when the dollar is broadly softer – maybe in
2Q27 – and when USD/ILS is threatening to run away sub-3.00.
Longer term, the trade-weighted ILS is very ﬁrm, and we suspect
Israeli authorities will struggle to tolerate much further strength.

Equity Research 
Sector Report | Networking｜Sep 29, 2026 
CPO Accelerates, Yet Stock Implications Vary

Overseas  
Technology

What’s New?  
Following our earlier notes of CPO/NPO Accelerates on Aug  17, we believe that Nvidia’s CPO scale-out switch progress is faster than 
expected. On the other hand, for NPO, due to slower than expected AWS T4 rollout, we revise our total OE shipments forecast to 6.5m/42m 
in 2027E/2028E respectively. We provide our view on recent development and key stocks below. 
Comments  
Nvidia’s Scale-out CPO:  Following Nvidia’s Aug ‑14 announcement, Spectrum ‑X CPO switches have entered mass production. To 
support the ramp, TSMC has expanded CPO testing/inspection capacity, notably adding Insertion 2/3 equipment. Key components such 
as FAU are also progressing well, helped by improving multi ‑plug/unplug yield rates. At the system level, we believe major component 
suppliers are already on site, enabling cleanroom ‑level system integration from early Sep t. We now forecast Nvidia’s CPO switch 
shipments at 15k/16k/30k/100k for 4Q26/1Q27/2Q27/2027. 
Nvidia & AWS Scale-up NPO: Per our AI Platform Value Shift report on Aug 6, NVL576‑based scale‑up CPO/NPO systems are expected 
to begin adoption in 2H27E, with two switch boards per NVSwitch tray, each board carrying four ASICs, paired with four 3.2T OEs/ASIC. 
We reiterate our view that CPO (QM5 + 4 OEs) and NPO (QM5 + 4 OEs on sockets) are progressing in parallel, and our view is similar 
with Street expectations for full NPO adoption at NVL576 launch given NPO maturity. The next to watch is Feynman, which our supply  
chain research indicate is also being developed in both CPO and NPO variants; packaging ‑side visibility suggests CPO (QM6 with six 
6.4T OEs) remains on track. For AWS, given limited near‑term visibility on T4 tape‑out, we now shift our Trainium 4 launch to 1Q28. We 
forecast AWS OE consumption at 0m/12m units for 2H27E/2028E. Incorporating ASIC and AMD volumes, we now expect global scale‑up 
OE demand to reach 6.5m/42m in 2027E/2028E.  
Recommendations   
• Likes: 1) Lumentum (LITE Buy) on CPO acceleration which supports valuation, with CPO/NPO revenue contribution of 12%/26% in 
CY27/CY28; 2) Browave (3163 TT) & Bizlink (3665 TT Buy)  on CPO’s shuffle box of ~8x content vs. NPO ’s, with production to 
accelerate in 1Q27E. 3) Semtech (SMTC Buy) on TIA/Driver for NPO, despite our downward revision, due to tight supply.  
• Dislikes: Largan (3008 TT) as our analysis indicates EPS contribution of NT$20/NT$97 in CY27/CY28, assuming shipments begin 
in 2H27, suggesting implied 20x CY28 valuation which is not particularly cheap, with further risk on market share given the incumbent 
supplier’s ongoing yield improvements and a growing number of new entrants.  
Risks   
1) AI demand slowdown, 2) supply constraint, 3) competition.

### Page 1

GROWTH MOMENTUM TO SUSTAIN, 
EXTERNAL RISKS LINGER

India registered  strong growth at the start of 
FY27. Sustaining this performance will depend 
increasingly on how effectively it navigates a 
more uncertain global environment. Besides 
trade fragmentation, geopolitical tensions 
leading to higher energy prices, and shifts in 
international capital flows present key external 
risks.

India joined its Southeast Asian peers in 
posting an upside surprise on growth in 
1QFY27 (Apr -Jun26), up a firm 7.8%  yoy. few 
points of note:  
A) strong 1Q growth print reinforced the view 
that India’s domestic demand cycle was more 
resilient than initially assumed, with 
consumption, public capex and manufacturing 
providing a solid base despite external shocks.  
B) prevailing tailwinds by way of indirect tax 
relief in 4Q25, real rate buffer, and easier 
monetary conditions (earlier cuts), were also 
supportive;  
C) nominal GDP growth returned above 10% 
after two years on higher deflators, though 
some segments like manufacturing faced 
negative deflators as input prices rose at a 
faster pace than output due to commodity price 
increases and import intensity;  
D) Revisions to backdated numbers, to account 
for output PPI series, pushed up FY26 growth to 
7.8% (from 7.7% earlier). Two prior years were 
also revised.

www.dbs.com/insightsdirect/macro groupresc@dbs.com 
• High frequency indicators 
might keep  2QFY growth 
close to 7%. 
• Pace to moderate in 2HFY. 
• Retail inflation is likely to 
continue ris e and broaden  in 
scope, necessitating a tighter 
policy bias.  WPI likely to r ise 
by double-digits. 
• Special swap windows helped 
to stem bearish INR 
positioning. 
• Organic capital flows need to 
catch up.  
• Liquidity management is in 
focus, with a moderate 
correlation to inflation. 
• Twin-deficit risks are partly 
offset by stronger macro 
buffers.
